Experiments on LLaMA and LLaMA2 show that Squeeze10-LLM achieves state-of-the-art performance for sub-2bit weight-only quantization, improving average accuracy from 43% to 56% on six zero-shot classification tasks--a significant boost over existing PTQ methods. Our code will be released upon publication.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Squeeze10-LLM: Squeezing LLMs' Weights by 10 Times via a Staged Mixed-Precision Quantization Method" addresses the challenge of deploying large language models (LLMs) on resource-constrained devices.  It introduces Squeeze10-LLM, a staged mixed-precision post-training quantization (PTQ) framework. This framework aims to compress 16-bit LLMs by a factor of 10 by quantizing 80% of the weights to 1 bit and the remaining 20% to 4 bits, achieving an average of 1.6 bits per weight.  The key innovations include Post-Binarization Activation Robustness (PBAR), a weight significance metric that considers the impact of quantization on activations, and Full Information Activation Supervision (FIAS), a strategy to preserve full activation information during quantization to prevent error propagation.  Experiments on LLaMA and LLaMA2 models demonstrate improved performance compared to existing sub-2-bit weight-only quantization techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies primarily in the combination of PBAR and FIAS within a staged quantization framework. While mixed-precision quantization and activation-aware techniques are not entirely new, the specific implementation and integration presented in this paper are noteworthy. The idea of a post-binarization activation robustness metric to identify crucial weights is a sound approach. Similarly, the full information activation supervision aims to stabilize quantization by using the original activation values, thus mitigating cumulative quantization errors. The *staged* approach to quantization also provides a controlled manner of minimizing performance degradation.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to enable the deployment of LLMs on devices with limited resources. Achieving a 10x compression ratio with a minimal performance drop is a valuable contribution. The results reported, showing a substantial improvement over existing sub-2-bit methods, are compelling and address a practical problem. The improvement from 43% to 56% accuracy on zero-shot tasks is a significant performance increase.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of deploying LLMs on resource-constrained devices and motivates the need for ultra-low-bit quantization.
    *   **Well-Defined Method:** The Squeeze10-LLM framework, including the PBAR and FIAS techniques, is well-defined and explained.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method, showing significant improvements over existing approaches on LLaMA and LLaMA2 models. The ablation studies help to validate the importance of both PBAR and FIAS.
    *   **Reproducibility:**  The paper has reproducibility components like making code and datasets public.
    *   **Comprehensive results:** The zero-shot classification tasks chosen were representative of current SOTA LLM benchmarks.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The analysis of salient weight proportions and the effect of different high-bit quantization levels provides valuable insights.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalization:** While the results are impressive on LLaMA and LLaMA2, it's unclear how well Squeeze10-LLM would generalize to other LLM architectures. It would be ideal to have results for models like GPT.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical and effective method for compressing LLMs. The PBAR and FIAS techniques could inspire further research in activation-aware quantization and error propagation mitigation.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of 8 because the paper presents a novel and significant contribution to LLM quantization. The combination of PBAR and FIAS within the staged quantization framework is well-motivated and demonstrated to be effective through strong experimental results.  While the lack of generalization to different model families is limiting, the paper's clear problem definition, well-defined method, and compelling results make it a valuable contribution to the field. The gains of accuracy over the other ultra-low bit methods are really quite substantial. It presents a path to ultra-low bit inference while maintaining acceptable performance.

### Hybrid and Unitary Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models: Methods and Benchmarking under Resource Constraints
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18076v1)
- **Authors**: Haomin Qi, Zihan Dai, Chengbo Huang
- **Abstract**: Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) remains a computational bottleneck due to their scale and memory demands. This paper presents a comprehensive evaluation of parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) techniques, including LoRA, BOFT, LoRA-GA, and uRNN, and introduces a novel hybrid strategy that dynamically integrates BOFT's orthogonal stability with LoRA-GA's gradient-aligned rapid convergence. By computing per-layer adaptive updates guided by gradient norms, the hybrid method achieves superior convergence efficiency and generalization across diverse tasks. We also explore, for the first time, the adaptation of unitary RNN (uRNN) principles to transformer-based LLMs, enhancing gradient stability through structured unitary constraints. Empirical evaluations on four benchmarks -- GLUE, GSM8K, MT-Bench, and HumanEval -- using models ranging from 7B to 405B parameters demonstrate that our hybrid method consistently outperforms individual PEFT baselines, approaching full fine-tuning accuracy while reducing resource consumption by up to 2.1 times in training time and 50 percent in memory usage. These findings establish the hybrid approach as a practical and scalable fine-tuning solution for real-world deployment of LLMs under resource constraints.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a novel approach to fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) that combines the strengths of several existing parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) techniques. The core idea is a "hybrid" method that dynamically weights updates from LoRA-GA (gradient-aligned low-rank adaptation) and BOFT (butterfly orthogonal fine-tuning) on a per-layer basis, using gradient norms to determine the mixing coefficient. The authors also explore adapting unitary RNN (uRNN) principles, typically used in recurrent networks, to transformer-based LLMs by embedding structured unitary matrices into attention and feedforward layers. The paper evaluates the proposed hybrid and uRNN-enhanced fine-tuning methods on GLUE, GSM8K, MT-Bench, and HumanEval using models ranging from 7B to 405B parameters, demonstrating superior performance compared to individual PEFT baselines and approaching full fine-tuning accuracy with reduced resource consumption.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper demonstrates novelty on several fronts.

*   **Hybrid Fine-Tuning:** The dynamic combination of LoRA-GA and BOFT is a genuinely novel approach.  It's not simply an ensemble of existing methods; it introduces a mechanism (gradient norm-based weighting) to actively manage their influence during training, which is a significant contribution. The adaptive weighting balances the rapid convergence of LoRA-GA with the stable gradient propagation of BOFT.
*   **uRNNs in Transformers:**  Adapting unitary RNN principles to transformers is another original idea. Applying a technique designed for recurrent models to feed-forward layers is innovative and opens up a new avenue for stabilizing training in very deep transformer architectures. While the paper presents initial results, the concept is sound and worth further exploration.
*   **Comprehensive Benchmarking:** The paper contributes a very comprehensive benchmark of existing and new PEFT methods across a range of model sizes and diverse tasks. This is valuable to the community, providing an apples-to-apples comparison that is often missing in PEFT research.

**Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to make LLM fine-tuning more practical and scalable, particularly in resource-constrained settings. The results show a consistent improvement in performance, approaching full fine-tuning accuracy while significantly reducing training time and memory usage. This could lower the barrier to entry for researchers and practitioners who lack access to high-end computational resources.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-motivated:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing PEFT techniques and motivates the need for a hybrid approach.
*   **Technically Sound:** The proposed methods are well-explained, and the mathematical formulations are clear. The algorithms provided (Algorithms 1 and 2) are helpful for reproducibility.
*   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experimental setup is rigorous, and the results consistently demonstrate the effectiveness of the hybrid approach. The benchmark covers a diverse set of models and tasks.
*   **Resource Analysis:** The paper includes a valuable resource analysis (training time and memory usage), which is crucial for assessing the practicality of the proposed methods.
*   **Insightful Analysis:** The discussion of gradient norms and validation loss provides insights into the training dynamics of the different methods.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Exploration of uRNNs:** The uRNN adaptation, while novel, seems less fully explored compared to the hybrid method. The paper might benefit from more detailed analysis of the benefits and limitations of using unitary matrices in transformer layers. For example, which layers are most suitable for this adaptation, and how does it affect long-range dependencies?
*   **Parameter Tuning:** While the authors mention hyperparameter tuning, details of the tuning process are somewhat limited.  A sensitivity analysis of key hyperparameters (e.g., the learning rates, weighting factor ) would strengthen the results.
*   **Theoretical Analysis:** While the paper provides experimental evidence, a deeper theoretical analysis of why the hybrid method works so well would be valuable.  This could involve exploring the convergence properties of the combined update rule.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of PEFT research by highlighting the benefits of combining different techniques and by introducing the idea of dynamic, per-layer adaptation. The adaptation of uRNNs to transformers could also inspire further research in this area.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:**

The paper delivers solid technical contributions backed by strong experimental results. The hybrid fine-tuning approach is genuinely novel and addresses a significant challenge in LLM adaptation. The adaptation of uRNN to transformers is an interesting and novel idea that warrants further investigation. The comprehensive benchmarking of PEFT techniques is a valuable resource for the community. While there are some weaknesses, such as limited exploration of the uRNN and lack of a more detailed theoretical analysis, the strengths of the paper outweigh its weaknesses. The paper makes a significant contribution to the field and has the potential to be highly influential.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Understanding the Supply Chain and Risks of Large Language Model Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18105v1)
- **Authors**: Yujie Ma, Lili Quan, Xiaofei Xie, Qiang Hu, Jiongchi Yu, Yao Zhang, Sen Chen
- **Abstract**: The rise of Large Language Models (LLMs) has led to the widespread deployment of LLM-based systems across diverse domains. As these systems proliferate, understanding the risks associated with their complex supply chains is increasingly important. LLM-based systems are not standalone as they rely on interconnected supply chains involving pretrained models, third-party libraries, datasets, and infrastructure. Yet, most risk assessments narrowly focus on model or data level, overlooking broader supply chain vulnerabilities. While recent studies have begun to address LLM supply chain risks, there remains a lack of benchmarks for systematic research. To address this gap, we introduce the first comprehensive dataset for analyzing and benchmarking LLM supply chain security. We collect 3,859 real-world LLM applications and perform interdependency analysis, identifying 109,211 models, 2,474 datasets, and 9,862 libraries. We extract model fine-tuning paths, dataset reuse, and library reliance, mapping the ecosystem's structure. To evaluate security, we gather 1,555 risk-related issues-50 for applications, 325 for models, 18 for datasets, and 1,229 for libraries from public vulnerability databases. Using this dataset, we empirically analyze component dependencies and risks. Our findings reveal deeply nested dependencies in LLM applications and significant vulnerabilities across the supply chain, underscoring the need for comprehensive security analysis. We conclude with practical recommendations to guide researchers and developers toward safer, more trustworthy LLM-enabled systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LLMSCBench, a new comprehensive dataset designed to facilitate the analysis and benchmarking of security risks within the Large Language Model (LLM) supply chain. It covers a large collection of real-world LLM applications and identifies core components (models, datasets, libraries) and their interdependencies. The dataset also includes security-related issues and vulnerabilities sourced from public databases and repositories. The authors use LLMSCBench to conduct an empirical study to analyze dependencies and associated risks, revealing complex and nested dependencies, and significant vulnerabilities across the LLM supply chain. The paper concludes with practical implications and recommendations for enhancing security and trustworthiness in LLM-enabled systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the creation of LLMSCBench itself. While previous works have explored LLM security, they often focused on the model level or data level. LLMSCBench offers a more holistic view of the entire LLM supply chain by capturing the interdependencies between applications, models, datasets, and libraries. The comprehensive nature of the dataset, including identified risk issues, is a significant step forward. However, it is important to acknowledge that the *methodologies* used to construct the dataset (e.g., scraping, dependency analysis, risk identification via keyword search) are not entirely novel in themselves, as they have been used in prior software engineering and security research. The novelty lies in the *application* of these methods to the LLM domain and the creation of a large, consolidated resource.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from the increasing importance of securing LLM-based systems. As these systems become more integrated into real-world applications, understanding and mitigating their security risks is critical. LLMSCBench offers a valuable resource for researchers and developers to conduct in-depth analyses, benchmark existing systems, and develop new security and privacy practices. The findings regarding the complex dependencies and widespread vulnerabilities in the LLM supply chain underscore the urgency of addressing these issues. The actionable recommendations provided in the paper contribute directly to the development of safer, more secure, and trustworthy LLM-enabled systems.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Comprehensive dataset covering multiple LLM components and their dependencies.
    *   Identification of security and privacy risks across the LLM supply chain.
    *   Empirical study providing valuable insights into the structure and vulnerabilities of LLM systems.
    *   Practical implications and actionable recommendations for researchers and developers.
    *   The work addresses a critical gap in understanding supply chain risks beyond model-level vulnerabilities in LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on user-provided descriptions for model and dataset dependencies can limit the accuracy and completeness of the data.
    *   The risk identification process, while thorough, relies heavily on keyword searches and manual validation, which may miss subtle or less well-documented vulnerabilities. A fully automated process is very difficult but may be more thorough in the future.
    *   The external validity might be limited by focusing solely on LLM applications from Hugging Face. Other LLM platforms could have different dependency patterns and vulnerabilities.
    *   While the paper identifies many vulnerabilities, a detailed analysis of their exploitability and impact is lacking. The paper hints towards risk propagation but lacks concrete examples.

*   **Potential Influence:** LLMSCBench has the potential to become a widely used benchmark dataset in the LLM security research community. It can facilitate comparative studies, encourage the development of new security analysis techniques, and inform the design of more robust LLM systems. The findings presented in the paper are also likely to influence industry practices, prompting developers and organizations to prioritize supply chain security in their LLM deployments. The data could be utilized to create improved automated threat detection models.

*   **Justification of Score:**  Given the paper's novelty in constructing a large, comprehensive dataset for LLM supply chain security analysis, its significant implications for the field, its well-executed empirical study, and its practical recommendations, it warrants a high score. However, the weaknesses regarding data completeness, validation methods, and external validity necessitate a slightly lower score.

### Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning of 3D DDPM for MRI Image Generation Using Tensor Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18112v1)
- **Authors**: Binghua Li, Ziqing Chang, Tong Liang, Chao Li, Toshihisa Tanaka, Shigeki Aoki, Qibin Zhao, Zhe Sun
- **Abstract**: We address the challenge of parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) for three-dimensional (3D) U-Net-based denoising diffusion probabilistic models (DDPMs) in magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) image generation. Despite its practical significance, research on parameter-efficient representations of 3D convolution operations remains limited. To bridge this gap, we propose Tensor Volumetric Operator (TenVOO), a novel PEFT method specifically designed for fine-tuning DDPMs with 3D convolutional backbones. Leveraging tensor network modeling, TenVOO represents 3D convolution kernels with lower-dimensional tensors, effectively capturing complex spatial dependencies during fine-tuning with few parameters. We evaluate TenVOO on three downstream brain MRI datasets-ADNI, PPMI, and BraTS2021-by fine-tuning a DDPM pretrained on 59,830 T1-weighted brain MRI scans from the UK Biobank. Our results demonstrate that TenVOO achieves state-of-the-art performance in multi-scale structural similarity index measure (MS-SSIM), outperforming existing approaches in capturing spatial dependencies while requiring only 0.3% of the trainable parameters of the original model. Our code is available at: https://github.com/xiaovhua/tenvoo
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Tensor Volumetric Operator (TenVOO), a novel parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) method for 3D U-Net-based denoising diffusion probabilistic models (DDPMs) applied to MRI image generation.  TenVOO leverages tensor network modeling to represent 3D convolution kernels using lower-dimensional tensors, aiming to capture complex spatial dependencies during fine-tuning with fewer parameters. The method is evaluated on three brain MRI datasets (ADNI, PPMI, BraTS2021) using a DDPM pre-trained on a large UK Biobank dataset. The results demonstrate that TenVOO achieves state-of-the-art performance in multi-scale structural similarity index measure (MS-SSIM) while using a small fraction (0.3%) of the original model's trainable parameters.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in applying tensor network decompositions *specifically* to 3D convolutional kernels within DDPMs for MRI generation, addressing a gap in parameter-efficient representation of 3D convolution operations. While tensor networks are not entirely new in PEFT, their tailored application to this specific problem and architecture is a distinct contribution. Prior work mainly focused on 2D convolutions or transformer architectures. The distinction between TenVOO-L and TenVOO-Q, while a good exploration, is incrementally novel.

*   **Significance:**  The significance stems from the practical importance of efficiently fine-tuning large 3D models for medical imaging.  DDPMs are computationally expensive, and the ability to adapt them to new datasets with limited resources has significant clinical implications. The reported performance gains, particularly in MS-SSIM, suggest that TenVOO can effectively preserve spatial information, which is critical for medical image interpretation. Demonstrating superior performance against other PEFT methods (LoRA, LoKr, LoHa) on established MRI datasets strengthens this claim.
    *   The paper highlights a challenge common in transfer learning for medical imaging – models trained on one dataset may not generalize well to others due to subtle differences in imaging protocols or patient populations. TenVOO provides a way to address this challenge with limited resources.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly articulates the problem and proposes a well-motivated solution.
    *   The experimental setup is rigorous, using established datasets and evaluation metrics.
    *   Ablation studies are performed to understand the impact of key parameters (rank).
    *   The comparisons to state-of-the-art PEFT methods provide a strong baseline.
    *   The code is publicly available, promoting reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The analysis of results on the PPMI dataset is somewhat weak. The argument that Parkinson's MRI scans have "minimal structural variations" should be supported with more concrete evidence or references. It feels like a post-hoc explanation for why TenVOO performed less well there.
    *   While MS-SSIM is important, the paper could benefit from including a measure of diagnostic accuracy or clinical utility in the evaluation. Demonstrating that improved MS-SSIM translates to better disease detection, segmentation, or other clinically relevant tasks would significantly increase the impact.
    *   The paper focuses heavily on the methodological contribution (TenVOO). A deeper discussion of the limitations of DDPMs for medical image generation *in general* could be helpful. Are there specific types of artifacts or distortions that DDPMs struggle with, and how might TenVOO address these?

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical and effective method for adapting large 3D models for medical imaging.  The parameter efficiency and demonstrated performance could encourage wider adoption of DDPMs in clinical settings. However, realizing this influence hinges on further validation of clinical utility.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid and novel contribution to parameter-efficient fine-tuning for medical image generation. The method is well-motivated, rigorously evaluated, and shows promising results. However, the lack of a direct link to clinical utility and the somewhat weak analysis of PPMI results slightly detract from its overall impact. While the approach effectively maintains structural similarity, the practical implications of this improvement require further examination.

### Policy Disruption in Reinforcement Learning:Adversarial Attack with Large Language Models and Critical State Identification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18113v1)
- **Authors**: Junyong Jiang, Buwei Tian, Chenxing Xu, Songze Li, Lu Dong
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) has achieved remarkable success in fields like robotics and autonomous driving, but adversarial attacks designed to mislead RL systems remain challenging. Existing approaches often rely on modifying the environment or policy, limiting their practicality. This paper proposes an adversarial attack method in which existing agents in the environment guide the target policy to output suboptimal actions without altering the environment. We propose a reward iteration optimization framework that leverages large language models (LLMs) to generate adversarial rewards explicitly tailored to the vulnerabilities of the target agent, thereby enhancing the effectiveness of inducing the target agent toward suboptimal decision-making. Additionally, a critical state identification algorithm is designed to pinpoint the target agent's most vulnerable states, where suboptimal behavior from the victim leads to significant degradation in overall performance. Experimental results in diverse environments demonstrate the superiority of our method over existing approaches.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ARCS, a novel adversarial attack framework for reinforcement learning (RL) systems. Unlike existing attacks that often require direct access to the environment or policy, ARCS works by strategically influencing a target RL agent through interactions with other agents in the environment, thereby manipulating the victim policy towards suboptimal behaviors *without* modifying the environment itself.  ARCS leverages two key components: 1) an LLM-guided reward iteration optimization module to generate customized adversarial reward functions tailored to the target agent's weaknesses, and 2) a critical state identification mechanism to pinpoint vulnerable states where suboptimal actions by the victim significantly impact overall performance. The adversarial reward optimization framework allows the attacker to adapt its strategy to different victim policies and achieve stronger, more targeted attacks. Experiments across various MuJoCo and autonomous driving environments demonstrate ARCS's superiority over existing adversarial policy training methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a contribution to the field of adversarial RL. The core idea of using LLMs to generate adversarial rewards and focusing on critical states is interesting. However, the claims of novelty and significance need to be evaluated critically.

*   **Novelty:** The use of LLMs to generate reward functions is a relatively recent trend, although not entirely novel, as prior works may have explored similar directions in different contexts.  The main claim to novelty lies in combining LLM-generated rewards with a critical state identification mechanism within an adversarial RL setting *without direct environment manipulation*. It does extend previous work in multi-agent adversarial RL by introducing adaptivity and task-specific guidance through the LLM reward function. However, the technical details need to be scrutinized for originality, particularly the reward generation and critical state selection algorithms.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper hinges on the practical applicability of the attack and the extent of its impact on real-world RL systems. While the experiments demonstrate improved attack success rates in simulated environments, it's crucial to consider the assumptions made about the environment and victim policy. The extent to which these findings translate to complex, real-world systems, particularly where defenses against adversarial attacks exist, should be further validated. The impact of successfully attacking the target agent by other existing agents can be highly detrimental in several real-world scenarios, and it is important to conduct a rigorous analysis to prevent any unintended consequences. The critical state identification method needs careful examination to see if the identified states transfer to real-world scenarios where state distribution may change.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The black-box nature of the attack makes it potentially applicable to a wider range of scenarios where direct access to the environment or policy is limited.
    *   The LLM-guided reward function allows for more adaptive and targeted attacks compared to fixed or generic objectives.
    *   The combination of reward optimization and critical state identification seems promising.
    *   The experiments cover a reasonably diverse set of simulated environments.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on LLMs introduces a dependence on the quality and cost of LLM services, which may be a barrier to adoption.
    *   The transferability of the learned adversarial policies and critical states to different environments or victim policies may be limited. More analysis on the generalization capabilities is needed.
    *   The practical feasibility of the attack in highly defended RL systems is uncertain.
    *   The evaluation focuses primarily on attack success rates, and other metrics such as stealthiness or robustness against defenses could be explored.

*   **Score Justification:** While the paper introduces a potentially valuable approach to adversarial RL, the novelty and significance claims require careful consideration. It is more of an incremental contribution compared to other more disruptive research in the field. The dependence on LLMs and the lack of real-world validation limit its immediate impact. Considering the strengths and weaknesses, the paper is assigned a score of **6**. This indicates a paper with some novel elements and potential for future impact but requires further validation and addresses a specific, existing problem rather than providing a groundbreaking solution. The ARCS framework must have a careful security analysis before deployment, and more considerations of ethical implications are expected.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### NoCode-bench: A Benchmark for Evaluating Natural Language-Driven Feature Addition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18130v1)
- **Authors**: Le Deng, Zhonghao Jiang, Jialun Cao, Michael Pradel, Zhongxin Liu
- **Abstract**: Natural language-driven no-code development allows users to specify software functionality using natural language (NL) instead of editing source code, promising increased productivity and democratized development. Large language models (LLMs) show potential in enabling this paradigm. In this context, software documentation acts as an NL specification for functionality. This work introduces NoCode-bench, a benchmark designed to evaluate LLMs on real-world NL-driven feature addition tasks, consisting of 634 tasks across 10 projects and 114k code changes. Each task pairs documentation updates with corresponding code implementations, validated by developer-written test cases. A subset of 114 high-quality, human-verified instances, NoCode-bench Verified, ensures reliable evaluation. Our experiments reveal that, despite high token usage, the best LLMs achieve a task success rate of only 15.79%, highlighting challenges in cross-file editing, codebase understanding, and tool calling. These findings indicate that LLMs are not yet ready for fully NL-driven no-code development. NoCode-bench lays the foundation for future advances in this area.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "NoCode-bench: A Benchmark for Evaluating Natural Language-Driven Feature Addition":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NoCode-bench, a new benchmark specifically designed to evaluate the performance of large language models (LLMs) in the context of natural language (NL)-driven no-code development.  This benchmark focuses on the task of feature addition, where LLMs are tasked with generating code changes based on user-facing documentation modifications. The dataset comprises 634 real-world instances from 10 open-source Python projects. The authors outline a five-phase pipeline for creating the benchmark, aiming for broad coverage and realistic development scenarios. They also curate a human-validated subset, NoCode-bench Verified, for lightweight and reliable evaluation.  Experiments using state-of-the-art LLMs show that current models struggle with this task, highlighting challenges such as cross-file editing, codebase understanding, and tool calling.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable and novel contribution by creating a benchmark specifically targeting NL-driven feature addition in no-code development.  Existing benchmarks in software engineering often focus on bug fixing or general issue resolution, lacking the specific focus on feature addition based on documentation changes.  The construction pipeline, particularly the use of release notes as a starting point, is a novel approach to instance collection, potentially reducing label noise compared to mining GitHub issues.

*   **Significance:** The benchmark addresses a critical gap in evaluating LLMs for a relevant software engineering task. Feature addition is a significant part of software maintenance, and no-code approaches hold promise for democratizing software development. By creating a benchmark based on real-world documentation changes and validated against test cases, the paper provides a realistic and objective measure of LLMs' capabilities in this area. The analysis of LLM failure modes (cross-file editing, codebase understanding, tool-calling) is also a valuable contribution, pointing towards areas for future research and model improvement.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined benchmark:** The five-phase pipeline for benchmark construction is clearly described and systematic.
    *   **Realistic task:** The focus on feature addition from documentation changes simulates a realistic no-code development scenario.
    *   **Human-validated subset:** The NoCode-bench Verified subset addresses concerns about task clarity and evaluation accuracy, providing a reliable resource for lightweight evaluation.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The experiments evaluate several state-of-the-art LLMs and analyze their performance using multiple metrics.
    *   **Identification of challenges:** The failure analysis provides valuable insights into the limitations of current LLMs in this domain.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited language coverage:** The benchmark is currently limited to Python projects with structured release notes. While this ensures quality, it restricts the generalizability of the findings to other languages and ecosystems.
    *   **Dependency on test cases:**  The benchmark relies on the availability of developer-written test cases for evaluation. While this supports objective evaluation, it may not always be feasible in real-world scenarios. The creation of automated test case generation models would be a valuable extension.
    *   **Reliance on particular toolchains:** The environment construction relies on Anaconda. While this is reasonable for the Python ecosystem, it limits portability to other language ecosystems.

*   **Impact:** This paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of LLM-based software engineering by providing a new benchmark for evaluating no-code feature addition. It highlights the limitations of current LLMs in this domain and provides valuable insights into the challenges that need to be addressed for future progress. It will likely be used by researchers to develop and evaluate new LLM-based techniques for no-code development. The benchmark will likely spur additional research in improving cross-file editing capabilities, codebase understanding, and tool calling abilities of LLMs.

*   **Justification for Score:**  While the paper presents a well-constructed benchmark with a novel task definition and a reasonable evaluation strategy, it is somewhat limited by its focus on the Python ecosystem and its dependency on existing test cases. The lack of experiments for more complex models due to cost also limits its significance. However, the significant contribution to the understanding of LLM limitations for no-code development tasks, the quality of the benchmark construction and manual verification processes, and the realistic task formulation lead to a positive evaluation.

- **Score**: 7/10

### MathOPEval: A Fine-grained Evaluation Benchmark for Visual Operations of MLLMs in Mathematical Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18140v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoyuan Li, Moxin Li, Wenjie Wang, Rui Men, Yichang Zhang, Fuli Feng, Dayiheng Liu, Junyang Lin
- **Abstract**: Recent progress in Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) has enabled step-by-step multi-modal mathematical reasoning by performing visual operations based on the textual instructions. A promising approach uses code as an intermediate representation to precisely express and manipulate the images in the reasoning steps. However, existing evaluations focus mainly on text-only reasoning outputs, leaving the MLLM's ability to perform accurate visual operations via code largely unexplored. This work takes a first step toward addressing that gap by evaluating MLLM's code-based capabilities in multi-modal mathematical reasoning.Specifically, our framework focuses on two key evaluation aspects: (1) Multi-modal Code Generation (MCG) evaluates the model's ability to accurately understand and construct visualizations from scratch. (2) Multi-modal Code Editing (MCE) assesses the model's capacity for fine-grained operations, which include three types: Deletion, Modification and Annotation. To evaluate the above tasks, we incorporate a dataset that covers the five most popular types of mathematical figures, including geometric diagrams, function plots, and three types of statistical charts, to provide a comprehensive and effective measurement of existing MLLMs. Our experimental evaluation involves nine mainstream MLLMs, and the results reveal that existing models still lag significantly behind human performance in performing fine-grained visual operations.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "MATHOPEVAL: A FINE-GRAINED EVALUATION BENCHMARK FOR VISUAL OPERATIONS OF MLLMS IN MATHEMATICAL REASONING":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MATHOPEVAL, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the ability of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to perform visual operations in the context of mathematical reasoning. The benchmark focuses on two key aspects: Multi-modal Code Generation (MCG), which assesses the model's ability to create code from visual inputs, and Multi-modal Code Editing (MCE), which evaluates the model's capacity to modify code to reflect changes in visual representations. The dataset covers five common mathematical figure types (geometric diagrams, function plots, and three statistical charts) and incorporates deletion, modification, and annotation operations. The authors tested nine mainstream MLLMs and found significant performance gaps compared to human-level capabilities, indicating that existing models struggle with fine-grained visual operations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its focus on code-based evaluation for visual operations. While existing benchmarks often assess MLLMs' overall reasoning abilities, MATHOPEVAL delves into the more granular aspect of visual manipulation through code as an intermediate representation. This is a valuable contribution because it provides a more precise way to assess the visual capabilities of MLLMs, rather than simply looking at the final output. The emphasis on code generation and editing is a distinguishing factor.

*   **Significance:** The paper has significant implications for the development of more robust and reliable MLLMs for mathematical and scientific applications. By highlighting the current limitations in visual operations, the benchmark directs future research towards improving the ability of MLLMs to accurately interpret and manipulate visual information. This is particularly important as MLLMs are increasingly used in domains where visual representations are central to the problem-solving process. Furthermore, the comprehensive dataset covering various figure types and operations provides a valuable resource for researchers working in this area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined Tasks:** MCG and MCE are clearly defined, and the breakdown of MCE into deletion, modification, and annotation provides a structured approach to evaluation.
    *   **Comprehensive Dataset:** The inclusion of diverse mathematical figures enhances the benchmark's generalizability and practical relevance. The size of the dataset is also considerable.
    *   **Focus on Visual Operations:** The emphasis on visual operations via code is a strong point, allowing for a more interpretable and fine-grained assessment of model capabilities.
    *   **Evaluation Strategy:**  The use of Chain-of-Thought for evaluation, particularly the automated evaluation, is a pragmatic approach.
    *   **Human evaluation demonstrates reliability:** The 92% agreement between human annotators and automated evaluation builds confidence in the dataset and the method.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Code Types:** The paper mentions Python and LaTeX as code types, but the focus seems primarily on Python (matplotlib, specifically). While justified, it may limit the scope of the benchmark.
    *   **Dependency on LLM Evaluator:** The reliance on another LLM (DeepSeek-V3) for evaluation, while innovative, may introduce biases and uncertainties. The 92% agreement helps alleviate this, but it's still a point to consider.
    *   **Generalization Beyond Mathematical Contexts:** The benchmark is specifically designed for mathematical reasoning. While the focus on visual operations is generally applicable, the types of operations and visualizations are tailored to this domain.
    *   **The prompting techniques used could be further investigated:** While Direct, CoT, DCoT, and VoT were investigated, there may be room for future investigation into prompting strategies or more specialized prompts for these visual tasks.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in the following ways:

    *   **Driving Model Development:** It provides a clear target for improving the visual reasoning capabilities of MLLMs.
    *   **Informing Evaluation Practices:** It promotes the use of code-based evaluation as a valuable tool for assessing visual understanding.
    *   **Facilitating Comparative Analysis:** The benchmark enables researchers to compare the performance of different MLLMs on visual operations.

*   **Rigorous Rationale for Score:** The score is assigned considering the paper's novel perspective on visual reasoning evaluation, its comprehensive dataset, and its potential impact on MLLM development. The identified limitations, such as dependency on LLM evaluator and specific focus on mathematical contexts, contribute to the final score.

**Score: 7.5**

The paper presents a valuable and well-executed study with a clear focus and significant potential impact. The novelty of using code as an intermediate representation for evaluating visual operations in MLLMs is significant. The identified weaknesses, primarily related to the reliance on LLM evaluation and niche task may limit a higher rating.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### HIVMedQA: Benchmarking large language models for HIV medical decision support
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18143v1)
- **Authors**: Gonzalo Cardenal Antolin, Jacques Fellay, Bashkim Jaha, Roger Kouyos, Niko Beerenwinkel, Diane Duroux
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are emerging as valuable tools to support clinicians in routine decision-making. HIV management is a compelling use case due to its complexity, including diverse treatment options, comorbidities, and adherence challenges. However, integrating LLMs into clinical practice raises concerns about accuracy, potential harm, and clinician acceptance. Despite their promise, AI applications in HIV care remain underexplored, and LLM benchmarking studies are scarce. This study evaluates the current capabilities of LLMs in HIV management, highlighting their strengths and limitations. We introduce HIVMedQA, a benchmark designed to assess open-ended medical question answering in HIV care. The dataset consists of curated, clinically relevant questions developed with input from an infectious disease physician. We evaluated seven general-purpose and three medically specialized LLMs, applying prompt engineering to enhance performance. Our evaluation framework incorporates both lexical similarity and an LLM-as-a-judge approach, extended to better reflect clinical relevance. We assessed performance across key dimensions: question comprehension, reasoning, knowledge recall, bias, potential harm, and factual accuracy. Results show that Gemini 2.5 Pro consistently outperformed other models across most dimensions. Notably, two of the top three models were proprietary. Performance declined as question complexity increased. Medically fine-tuned models did not always outperform general-purpose ones, and larger model size was not a reliable predictor of performance. Reasoning and comprehension were more challenging than factual recall, and cognitive biases such as recency and status quo were observed. These findings underscore the need for targeted development and evaluation to ensure safe, effective LLM integration in clinical care.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary, critical evaluation, and score for the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "HIVMedQA: Benchmarking large language models for HIV medical decision support" introduces a new benchmark, HIVMedQA, for evaluating the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in the context of HIV patient management.  The authors curate a dataset of HIV-related questions, developed and validated with an infectious disease physician,  and use it to assess the strengths and limitations of ten LLMs (seven general-purpose and three medically specialized). They evaluate model performance using a combination of lexical similarity metrics and an LLM-as-a-judge approach (MedGPT), focusing on question comprehension, reasoning, knowledge recall, bias, potential harm, and factual accuracy.  The study reveals that Gemini 2.5 Pro consistently outperforms other models, that medically fine-tuned models don't always surpass general-purpose models, and that reasoning and comprehension pose greater challenges than knowledge recall. The authors also highlight the susceptibility of LLMs to cognitive biases and emphasize the superiority of LLM-as-a-judge evaluation for capturing clinical accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of this paper lies primarily in the creation of the HIVMedQA benchmark itself. While other benchmarks exist for evaluating LLMs in medical domains, HIVMedQA provides a targeted dataset specifically focused on the complexities of HIV management. This focus is important given the chronic nature of the disease, the importance of individualized treatment strategies, and the potential for AI to support clinical decision-making in this area. The inclusion of questions designed to assess susceptibility to cognitive biases is another notable aspect, although the lack of observed bias suggests potential limitations in the benchmark's capacity to actually reveal it.
The use of both lexical methods and LLM-as-judge, coupled with their detailed prompts to evaluate clinical nuance, also strengthens the paper.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this study stems from its comprehensive evaluation of LLMs in a clinically relevant and challenging domain. The findings highlight critical limitations of current LLMs, particularly in reasoning, comprehension, and susceptibility to cognitive biases, even in models that are medically fine-tuned. This provides valuable insights for researchers and developers working to improve the reliability and trustworthiness of LLMs for clinical decision support. The paper also underscores the importance of robust evaluation strategies that go beyond simple factual recall and consider clinical reasoning, potential harm, and bias. The finding that specialized models do not consistently outperform generalist models is also significant, challenging the assumption that domain-specific fine-tuning is always necessary or sufficient.
The comparison of proprietary and open models is important, but the difficulty to replicate proprietary models limits reproducibility.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined Benchmark:** The HIVMedQA benchmark is a valuable contribution, offering a standardized dataset and evaluation metrics for assessing LLMs in HIV management.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study covers a wide range of LLMs and evaluation dimensions, providing a thorough assessment of their capabilities and limitations.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The authors employ a combination of lexical similarity metrics and an LLM-as-a-judge approach, providing a more nuanced and comprehensive evaluation.
    *   **Clinically Relevant Findings:** The study highlights critical limitations of current LLMs in clinical reasoning, comprehension, and susceptibility to cognitive biases.
    *   The authors provide open-source data for replication.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Cognitive Bias Assessment:** While the study attempts to assess susceptibility to cognitive biases, the lack of observed bias suggests that the benchmark may not be sufficiently sensitive to capture these effects. The modifications to original questions may not have been sufficiently subtle to trigger cognitive biases in the models.
    *   **Dependence on GPT-4 for Evaluation:** The reliance on GPT-4 as the "judge" introduces a potential source of bias and limits the generalizability of the findings. While the authors attempt to mitigate this through prompt engineering and careful evaluation, it is important to acknowledge this limitation.
    *   **General limitations:** as stated previously, closed source models cannot be fully assessed or replicated, and single-turn dialog.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to influence future research and development in the field of medical LLMs. The HIVMedQA benchmark will provide a valuable resource for evaluating and comparing different models. The findings will also inform the development of more robust and reliable LLMs for clinical decision support, emphasizing the importance of reasoning, comprehension, and bias mitigation.

**Score:** 7/10

**Rationale:** The paper makes a valuable contribution through the introduction of the HIVMedQA benchmark and its comprehensive evaluation of LLMs in HIV management. While the study has some limitations, particularly in the scope of cognitive bias assessment and the dependence on GPT-4 for evaluation, the findings are clinically relevant and provide valuable insights for the field. The paper's strengths in methodology and evaluation outweigh its weaknesses. The novel benchmark and open data will likely make a strong contribution to future work on LLMs in healthcare. The paper is not groundbreaking enough to warrant a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### When Noisy Labels Meet Class Imbalance on Graphs: A Graph Augmentation Method with LLM and Pseudo Label
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18153v1)
- **Authors**: Riting Xia, Rucong Wang, Yulin Liu, Anchen Li, Xueyan Liu, Yan Zhang
- **Abstract**: Class-imbalanced graph node classification is a practical yet underexplored research problem. Although recent studies have attempted to address this issue, they typically assume clean and reliable labels when processing class-imbalanced graphs. This assumption often violates the nature of real-world graphs, where labels frequently contain noise. Given this gap, this paper systematically investigates robust node classification for class-imbalanced graphs with noisy labels. We propose GraphALP, a novel Graph Augmentation framework based on Large language models (LLMs) and Pseudo-labeling techniques. Specifically, we design an LLM-based oversampling method to generate synthetic minority nodes, producing label-accurate minority nodes to alleviate class imbalance. Based on the class-balanced graphs, we develop a dynamically weighted pseudo-labeling method to obtain high-confidence pseudo labels to reduce label noise ratio. Additionally, we implement a secondary LLM-guided oversampling mechanism to mitigate potential class distribution skew caused by pseudo labels. Experimental results show that GraphALP achieves superior performance over state-of-the-art methods on class-imbalanced graphs with noisy labels.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of class-imbalanced graph node classification in the presence of noisy labels, a practical but often overlooked scenario. It proposes GraphALP, a novel graph augmentation framework that integrates Large Language Models (LLMs) and pseudo-labeling. GraphALP leverages LLMs to generate synthetic minority nodes, thereby alleviating class imbalance and ensuring label accuracy. It then employs a dynamically weighted pseudo-labeling method to reduce label noise. A secondary LLM-guided oversampling step further mitigates potential class distribution skew introduced by pseudo-labeling. Experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate GraphALP's superior performance compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in explicitly tackling the combined challenge of class imbalance and noisy labels in graph node classification. While existing work has addressed these issues individually, the paper's systematic investigation of their synergistic interaction is valuable. The integration of LLMs for synthetic node generation is also a relatively novel approach in this specific context, moving beyond traditional oversampling techniques that can propagate label noise. The dynamically weighted pseudo-labeling and secondary LLM oversampling for label skew mitigation are also unique contributions.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a highly practical problem with potential real-world impact. Many graph-based applications, such as fraud detection and citation networks, suffer from both class imbalance and noisy labels. A robust solution like GraphALP could significantly improve performance in these scenarios.  The use of LLMs to address these challenges is a timely and potentially impactful direction, reflecting the growing importance of LLMs in various machine learning tasks.

**Strengths:**

*   **Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly defines and motivates the problem of class imbalance and noisy labels on graphs, highlighting the limitations of existing approaches.
*   **Methodological Soundness:** GraphALP is well-designed, with clear justifications for each component (LLM-based oversampling, weighted pseudo-labeling, and secondary oversampling). The use of LLMs to generate synthetic data addresses some potential drawbacks of traditional techniques.
*   **Experimental Evaluation:**  The experiments are comprehensive, using multiple real-world datasets and comparing GraphALP against a range of strong baselines. Ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component. Visualization helps to understand the efficacy of the method.
*   **Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to understand.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** While the paper mentions efficiency and demonstrates faster runtime than GraphDAO, the incorporation of LLMs inherently increases the computational cost compared to methods without LLMs. This limitation should be discussed more explicitly. Further detailed analyses of the compute are encouraged.
*   **LLM dependency:** The reliance on LLMs introduces a dependency that might be problematic given the rapid evolution of LLMs and their associated APIs. A more detailed discussion on the LLM model selection, the prompts used, and their sensitivity would be beneficial. The prompts should also be in the appendix.
*   **Generalizability of Prompts:** The prompts used to guide LLM data generation could be sensitive to specific datasets and tasks. How these prompts were selected and if the strategy used is generalizable to new datasets could also be included.
*   **Evaluation of synthetic label quality:** How much better is it to synthesize labels as compared to adding the noise? In the end, is the synthesis better?

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in graph learning, particularly in addressing realistic scenarios with noisy and imbalanced data. The use of LLMs for data augmentation in graphs could inspire further exploration in this direction.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing a practically relevant problem. The combination of LLMs and pseudo-labeling in GraphALP is novel and the experimental results are promising. The code and models should be shared with the community.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Decoupling Knowledge and Reasoning in LLMs: An Exploration Using Cognitive Dual-System Theory
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18178v1)
- **Authors**: Mutian Yang, Jiandong Gao, Ji Wu
- **Abstract**: While large language models (LLMs) leverage both knowledge and reasoning during inference, the capacity to distinguish between them plays a pivotal role in model analysis, interpretability, and development. Inspired by dual-system cognitive theory, we propose a cognition attribution framework to decouple the contribution of knowledge and reasoning. In particular, the cognition of LLMs is decomposed into two distinct yet complementary phases: knowledge retrieval (Phase 1) and reasoning adjustment (Phase 2). To separate these phases, LLMs are prompted to generate answers under two different cognitive modes, fast thinking and slow thinking, respectively. The performance under different cognitive modes is analyzed to quantify the contribution of knowledge and reasoning. This architecture is employed to 15 LLMs across 3 datasets. Results reveal: (1) reasoning adjustment is domain-specific, benefiting reasoning-intensive domains (e.g., mathematics, physics, and chemistry) and potentially imparing knowledge-intensive domains. (2) Parameter scaling improves both knowledge and reasoning, with knowledge improvements being more pronounced. Additionally, parameter scaling make LLMs reasoning significantly more prudent, while moderately more intelligent. (3) Knowledge primarily resides in lower network layers, while reasoning operates in higher layers. Our framework not only helps understand LLMs from a "decoupling" perspective, but also provides new insights into existing research, including scaling laws, hierarchical knowledge editing, and limitations of small-model reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Decoupling Knowledge and Reasoning in LLMs: An Exploration Using Cognitive Dual-System Theory" introduces a cognition attribution framework inspired by dual-system cognitive theory to disentangle the contributions of knowledge and reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs). The framework decomposes LLM inference into two phases: knowledge retrieval (Phase 1) and reasoning adjustment (Phase 2).  The authors prompt LLMs using two distinct cognitive modes: "fast thinking" (relying mainly on Phase 1) and "slow thinking" (employing both phases). By analyzing the performance differences, they quantify the contributions of knowledge and reasoning. They apply this framework to 15 LLMs across 3 datasets, revealing domain-specific variations in reasoning, the differential impact of parameter scaling on knowledge vs. reasoning, and a hierarchical localization of these processes within network layers. They find that small LLMs overthink more than correct during reasoning. The paper uses the framework to provide insights into scaling laws, hierarchical knowledge editing, and limitations of small-model reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its application of dual-system cognitive theory to analyze LLM inference.  While prior work has explored knowledge and reasoning in LLMs, this work offers a novel methodology to separate and quantify their contributions *without requiring manual annotations or complex prompting strategies*. This is done by leveraging the inherent properties of fast vs. slow thinking in language models.  The approach of partitioning the LLM's cognitive functions and eliciting separate behavior via carefully-crafted prompts constitutes a significant methodological contribution.

**Significance:** The findings offer several significant insights:

*   **Domain Specificity of Reasoning:** Quantifying how reasoning's contribution varies across domains (benefitting reasoning-intensive domains but potentially hindering knowledge-rich domains) provides valuable guidance for task selection and model fine-tuning.
*   **Differential Impact of Scaling:** The observation that parameter scaling disproportionately improves knowledge retrieval compared to reasoning is crucial for understanding the nature of emergent abilities in LLMs. The claim that scaling makes LLMs "more prudent" is intriguing and empirically supported.
*   **Hierarchical Localization:** The preliminary evidence of knowledge retrieval residing in lower layers and reasoning in higher layers supports hierarchical knowledge editing.
* The study highlights limitations in the reasoning capacity of smaller LLMs by revealing their proneness to overthinking, providing a deeper comprehension of the constraints encountered by models with restricted scales.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-Defined Framework:** The proposed cognition attribution architecture is clearly articulated and easy to understand.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The framework is rigorously tested on a diverse set of LLMs and datasets.
*   **Actionable Insights:** The findings have practical implications for model development, fine-tuning, and task selection.
* Methodologically sound prompting techniques effectively separates the LLM’s responses into distinctly defined cognitive modes.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Simplified Model:** The dual-system cognitive theory is a complex framework, and applying it to LLMs inevitably involves some simplification. The LLM is not necessarily a perfect mapping of how human cognition works. It is arguable whether the two phase framework can capture all nuances of LLMs, thus further investigation will be required.
*   **Multiple-Choice Limitation:** The evaluation is primarily based on multiple-choice QA, which may not fully capture the complexities of open-ended reasoning or generation tasks.
*  **Layer localization is not conclusive:** The CKA result is suggestive, but further analysis with other approaches could solidify this finding.
* The paper only demonstrates the application of the framework, further investigation of specific phenomena using the framework is required to evaluate its efficacy.

**Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence several areas:

*   **LLM Interpretability:** By providing a methodology to dissect the contributions of knowledge and reasoning, it can enhance the interpretability of LLMs.
*   **Model Optimization:** The insights into scaling laws and hierarchical localization can guide model design and optimization strategies.
*   **Educational Applications:** The findings related to small-model reasoning limitations could inform the development of more effective training methods for smaller LLMs.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:** The paper offers a novel and empirically-supported framework for analyzing LLM cognition, yielding valuable insights into the interplay of knowledge and reasoning. While the simplified application of dual-system theory and reliance on multiple-choice QA represent limitations, the paper's strengths in methodology, empirical validation, and actionable insights warrant a positive evaluation. The paper also provides additional insights to certain prior research. However, the lack of conclusive evidence for certain results requires additional investigation. The framework shows efficacy and has a high potential for application in the field. Further investigation would increase the validity and impact of the study.
- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### SCOPE: Stochastic and Counterbiased Option Placement for Evaluating Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18182v1)
- **Authors**: Wonjun Jeong, Dongseok Kim, Taegkeun Whangbo
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) can achieve inflated scores on multiple-choice tasks by exploiting inherent biases in option positions or labels, rather than demonstrating genuine understanding. This study introduces SCOPE, an evaluation framework designed to measure and mitigate such selection bias in a dataset-independent manner. By repeatedly invoking a null prompt that lacks semantic content, SCOPE estimates each model's unique position-bias distribution. It then redistributes the answer slot according to the inverse-bias distribution, thereby equalizing the lucky-rate, the probability of selecting the correct answer by chance. Furthermore, it prevents semantically similar distractors from being placed adjacent to the answer, thereby blocking near-miss guesses based on superficial proximity cues. Across multiple benchmark experiments, SCOPE consistently outperformed existing debiasing methods in terms of stable performance improvements and showed clearer confidence distributions over correct options. This framework thus offers a new standard for enhancing the fairness and reliability of LLM evaluations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SCOPE, a novel evaluation framework for Large Language Models (LLMs) designed to mitigate the impact of selection bias in multiple-choice question answering. SCOPE consists of two modules: Inverse-Positioning (IP) and Semantic-Spread (SS). IP estimates the model's intrinsic position bias by repeatedly prompting with semantically empty inputs and redistributes answer slots based on the inverse-bias distribution. SS suppresses near-miss guesses by ensuring semantically similar distractors are placed far from the correct answer, leveraging Sentence-BERT embeddings. The framework aims to provide a more reliable and fair evaluation of LLMs' true language understanding ability by reducing reliance on superficial cues or shortcut strategies. The authors present experimental results on MMLU and CSQA benchmarks, demonstrating SCOPE's effectiveness in mitigating selection bias and improving overall performance compared to existing debiasing methods. An ablation study dissects the contributions of each module.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of estimating a model's position bias through "null prompts" is novel and allows for dataset-independent bias assessment. Combining this with the semantic distancing of distractors creates a comprehensive framework. However, some of the techniques used (like inverse probability weighting or semantic embeddings) are individually well-established, although the *integration* and specific application to LLM debiasing are novel.

*   **Significance:** Selection bias is a crucial issue affecting the reliability and fairness of LLM evaluations. SCOPE tackles this problem with a well-reasoned and effective framework. By reducing reliance on superficial cues, SCOPE offers a more accurate assessment of LLMs' genuine language understanding ability. The practical implications of a fairer evaluation are substantial, potentially impacting model selection, development strategies, and downstream applications where robust performance is critical. The inclusion of theoretical guarantees strengthens the validity of the method.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Dataset-Independent Bias Estimation:** A key strength is the ability to estimate position bias without relying on the dataset itself, making the approach more generalizable.
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The combination of IP and SS modules addresses two significant sources of bias, leading to more robust evaluations.
    *   **Improved Performance and Interpretability:** The experiments demonstrate consistent performance improvements and provide insights into model behavior through the analysis of answer and distractor metric families.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The theoretical analysis provides a strong foundation for the framework and demonstrates its ability to mitigate position bias.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The paper includes a detailed reproducibility statement and provides code and data, which is a significant strength for the research community.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** The repeated prompting with null prompts may be computationally expensive for proprietary models with limited API access. The paper acknowledges this limitation and suggests potential solutions, but the cost remains a practical concern.
    *   **Surface-Level Biases:** The framework primarily focuses on position and semantic proximity biases. It does not address all potential biases, such as those related to input length, word frequency, or topic.
    *   **Dependency on Embedding Quality:** The effectiveness of the SS module depends on the quality of the Sentence-BERT embeddings. In domain-specific contexts, the embeddings may not accurately capture semantic similarity, which could limit the effectiveness of SS. The paper acknowledges this limitation.
    *   **Increase in Co-F:**  The methodology while encouraging correct responses also increases confident incorrect responses due to the encouragement of evidence based attempts to reduce bias from position.
    * **Limited Model Pool:** While the pool of models tested covers a relatively wide selection, it does not include more recent models that may behave significantly differently or demonstrate different biases.

*   **Potential Influence:** SCOPE has the potential to influence how LLMs are evaluated and developed. It offers a practical and theoretically grounded approach to mitigating selection bias, which could lead to more reliable and fair comparisons between models. The framework's interpretability also allows for a deeper understanding of model behavior and potential areas for improvement.

*   **Score:** 7.5

*   **Rationale:** SCOPE addresses a critical problem in LLM evaluation with a novel and well-designed framework. The dataset independent bias estimation and combination with semantically similar distractor handling are strong positives. It offers theoretical guarantees for performance and provides empirical evidence for effectiveness across various LLMs. The primary reasons for the score not being higher are the limitations regarding computational cost for proprietary models (affecting scalability), the dependence on the potentially limited generalizability of the embedding quality for niche topics and the potential for additional biases not addressed by the model, and the lack of a more expansive model set to provide a more broad overview of scope's performance. However, it is a significant contribution that improves fairness and reliability in LLM evaluation, making it a promising direction for future research.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Safeguarding RAG Pipelines with GMTP: A Gradient-based Masked Token Probability Method for Poisoned Document Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18202v1)
- **Authors**: San Kim, Jonghwi Kim, Yejin Jeon, Gary Geunbae Lee
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) enhances Large Language Models (LLMs) by providing external knowledge for accurate and up-to-date responses. However, this reliance on external sources exposes a security risk, attackers can inject poisoned documents into the knowledge base to steer the generation process toward harmful or misleading outputs. In this paper, we propose Gradient-based Masked Token Probability (GMTP), a novel defense method to detect and filter out adversarially crafted documents. Specifically, GMTP identifies high-impact tokens by examining gradients of the retriever's similarity function. These key tokens are then masked, and their probabilities are checked via a Masked Language Model (MLM). Since injected tokens typically exhibit markedly low masked-token probabilities, this enables GMTP to easily detect malicious documents and achieve high-precision filtering. Experiments demonstrate that GMTP is able to eliminate over 90% of poisoned content while retaining relevant documents, thus maintaining robust retrieval and generation performance across diverse datasets and adversarial settings.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces Gradient-based Masked Token Probability (GMTP), a novel method for safeguarding Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) pipelines against corpus poisoning attacks. GMTP identifies and filters out adversarially crafted documents by analyzing the masked token probability of key tokens, which are determined by examining gradients of the retriever's similarity function. Experiments on several datasets and adversarial settings demonstrate that GMTP effectively eliminates over 90% of poisoned content while preserving relevant documents, maintaining robust retrieval and generation performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a timely and crucial issue in the security of RAG systems: the vulnerability to corpus poisoning attacks. RAG's reliance on external knowledge makes it susceptible to malicious content injection, which can lead to harmful or misleading outputs.  GMTP presents a sensible defense mechanism leveraging the observation that adversarially crafted tokens often exhibit unnatural linguistic patterns that become apparent when masked.

*Strengths:*

*   **Novel Approach:** The method is based on a well-motivated idea that exploits the linguistic unnaturalness of adversarial tokens.  Using gradients to identify "key tokens" and then masking them provides a specific and targeted approach.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** Extensive experiments on multiple datasets (Natural Questions, HotpotQA, MS MARCO) and attack scenarios (PoisonedRAG, Phantom, AdvDecoding) validate the effectiveness of GMTP. The consistently high filtering rate and preserved retrieval performance are convincing.
*   **Robustness:** GMTP demonstrates robustness across different hyperparameter configurations and achieves high filtering rates, suggesting its practical applicability. Also, it's demonstrated to work with both DPR and Contriever which shows generality of method. It even works with alternative masking models like ROBERTa.
*   **Efficiency:** GMTP utilizes relatively lightweight computations compared to generation-based defense methods, making it more cost-effective. Demonstrates a strong speedup compared to other defence mechanisms.
*   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the GMTP methodology, experimental setup, and results.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Scope of Attacks:** The paper primarily focuses on attacks that involve injecting adversarially crafted tokens with unnatural linguistic patterns. It acknowledges that GMTP may struggle with detecting naturally crafted documents containing false or biased information.  The reliance on detecting "unnaturalness" inherently limits its effectiveness against more sophisticated attacks that minimize or eliminate this feature. The authors acknowledge in limitations section, however this makes it worth noting in a critical evaluation.
*   **Dependence on Key Token Identification:** The effectiveness of GMTP relies heavily on accurately identifying key tokens using gradient analysis. The sensitivity of gradient-based methods to small perturbations could potentially be exploited by attackers who are aware of the defense mechanism.
*   **Parameter Tuning:**  While GMTP demonstrates robustness, the performance can be affected by hyperparameter settings (N, M, lambda). Fine-tuning these parameters may be required for optimal performance in different domains or with different datasets.
*   **Evaluation Metric Bias:** The success of the proposed method is somewhat dependent on the performance on the evaluation metrics used. Some metrics like attack success rate rely on a specific model behaving in a certain way.
*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the application of masking techniques to detect adversarial tokens is somewhat novel, the underlying idea of using gradients for feature importance is not entirely new. Prior work like "GradMask" have exploited gradients for adversarial detection. The main differences is that this paper exploits that for retrieval augmentation.

*Significance:*

Despite these limitations, GMTP represents a significant contribution to the field by providing an effective and efficient defense against corpus poisoning attacks in RAG pipelines. It opens up opportunities for further research in developing more robust and adaptable defense mechanisms for knowledge-intensive NLP tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a strong, well-validated method, its limitations regarding more sophisticated attacks and the reliance on key token identification prevent it from receiving a higher score. The contribution is significant and practical, but it's not a groundbreaking paradigm shift. Moreover, the use of gradient analysis isn't entirely novel. Given these points, a score of 7 is justified.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Exploring the Impact of Instruction-Tuning on LLM's Susceptibility to Misinformation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18203v1)
- **Authors**: Kyubeen Han, Junseo Jang, Hongjin Kim, Geunyeong Jeong, Harksoo Kim
- **Abstract**: Instruction-tuning enhances the ability of large language models (LLMs) to follow user instructions more accurately, improving usability while reducing harmful outputs. However, this process may increase the model's dependence on user input, potentially leading to the unfiltered acceptance of misinformation and the generation of hallucinations.
